Efficient Inference for Inverse Reinforcement Learning
and Dynamic Discrete Choice Models
Lars van der Laan∗1,2, Aur´elien Bibaut2, and Nathan Kallus2,3
1Department of Statistics, University of Washington
2Netflix Research
3Cornell Tech, Cornell University
January 1, 2026
Abstract
Inverse reinforcement learning (IRL) and dynamic discrete choice (DDC) models explain
sequential decision-making by recovering reward functions that rationalize observed behaviour.
Flexible IRL methods typically rely on machine learning but provide no guarantees for valid
inference, while classical DDC approaches impose restrictive parametric specifications and often
require repeated dynamic programming. We develop a semiparametric framework for debiased
inverse reinforcement learning that yields statistically efficient inference for a broad class of
reward-dependent functionals in maximum-entropy IRL and Gumbel-shock DDC models. We
show that the log–behaviour policy acts as a pseudo-reward that point-identifies policy value
differences and, under a simple normalization, the reward itself. We then formalize these tar-
gets—including policy values under known and counterfactual softmax policies and functionals
of the normalized reward—as smooth functionals of the behaviour policy and transition ker-
nel, establish pathwise differentiability, and derive their efficient influence functions. Building
on this characterization, we construct automatic debiased machine-learning estimators that al-
low flexible nonparametric estimation of nuisance components while achieving √n-consistency,
asymptotic normality, and semiparametric efficiency. Our framework extends classical inference
for DDC models to nonparametric rewards and modern machine-learning tools, providing a
unified and computationally tractable approach to statistical inference in IRL.
1
Introduction
Behavioral data arise in fields such as robotics, human–computer interaction, healthcare, and eco-
nomics. Inverse reinforcement learning (IRL) has been used to model expert driving and robotic
control (e.g. Abbeel and Ng, 2004; Wulfmeier et al., 2016), user behaviour in interactive systems
∗Corresponding author: lvdlaan@uw.edu
1
arXiv:2512.24407v1 [cs.LG] 30 Dec 2025

(e.g. Chandramohan et al., 2011; Hossain et al., 2023), and clinical decision-making in critical care
(e.g. Yu et al., 2019; Bovenzi et al., 2025).
IRL offers a principled framework for interpreting
such data by recovering reward functions that rationalize observed behaviour. These recovered
rewards support counterfactual reasoning, allowing analysts to evaluate long-term outcomes under
alternative policies and to anticipate how behaviour may change with the environment.
A central difficulty is that IRL is inherently ill-posed: many reward functions can explain the
same behaviour (Ng et al., 1999; Ng and Russell, 2000; Cao et al., 2021; Skalse et al., 2023; Skalse and
Abate, 2024). Classical formulations address this by assuming agents act optimally with respect to
an unknown reward (Ng and Russell, 2000; Abbeel and Ng, 2004), but the resulting deterministic
policies often fail to reflect the variability observed in real behavioural data. Modern methods
instead assume approximate or entropy-regularized optimality, leading to stochastic policies that
better capture heterogeneity in decisions (Ziebart et al., 2008, 2010; Haarnoja et al., 2017).
A particularly influential formulation comes from dynamic discrete choice (DDC) models in eco-
nomics (Rust, 1987; Hotz and Miller, 1993; Aguirregabiria and Mira, 2010), where agents maximize
discounted utility subject to Gumbel shocks. This structure leads to a softmax policy that satisfies
a soft Bellman equation. Independently, maximum-entropy (MaxEnt) IRL (Ziebart et al., 2008;
Ziebart, 2010) derives the same policy form by augmenting reward maximization with entropy regu-
larization. Both literatures therefore arrive at a coherent model of approximately optimal stochastic
behaviour, replacing brittle argmax rules with a more stable softmax policy. Because many distinct
rewards induce the same softmax policy, the reward function is only partially identified from be-
haviour alone (Cao et al., 2021). Nevertheless, policy value differences can be recovered without full
reward identification (van der Laan et al., 2025c), thereby allowing for policy evaluation. To obtain
a uniquely defined reward, it is common to impose additional normalization constraints—such as
fixing the utility of a reference action or enforcing a zero-mean condition—which select a single
representative of the resulting equivalence class of rewards (Rust, 1987; Geng et al., 2020; van der
Laan et al., 2025c).
Inference on real-valued summaries of the reward function is central to IRL applications, in-
cluding counterfactual policy evaluation and analyses of agent behaviour (Kalouptsidi et al., 2021;
Zielnicki et al., 2025). Most prior work follows one of two paths: statistical inference within para-
metric DDC models (e.g. Rust, 1987; Hotz and Miller, 1993; Aguirregabiria and Mira, 2010), or
flexible machine-learning approaches to reward estimation that do not provide formal inference
guarantees (e.g. Abbeel and Ng, 2004; Ramachandran and Amir, 2007; Ziebart et al., 2008; Levine
et al., 2011; Ho and Ermon, 2016; Fu et al., 2018; Wulfmeier et al., 2016; Geng et al., 2020). Para-
metric approaches enable inference but rely on restrictive assumptions that risk misspecification.
Machine-learning methods offer flexibility and strong empirical performance, but directly applying
summary functionals to ML-based reward estimates can lead to bias and slow convergence. Conse-
quently, rigorous statistical inference in IRL settings with flexible, nonparametric reward estimation
largely remains an open problem.
2

1.1
Our contributions
We develop a general framework for debiased inverse reinforcement learning that enables efficient
statistical inference on reward-dependent functionals, most notably policy values. Our approach
employs debiased machine learning and accommodates flexible, nonparametric estimation of the
underlying components. This work builds on our companion paper van der Laan et al. (2025c),
which shows that, in softmax IRL, the true reward can be recovered from the log–behaviour policy
under an appropriate normalization. Here we extend these identification results by developing a
full inferential framework.
Our main contributions are as follows:
1. We show that a broad class of reward-dependent quantities admit natural representations
as smooth functionals of the log behavior policy and the transition kernel. This provides
a foundation for statistical inference in softmax IRL, in which reward-based estimands are
expressed directly in terms of observable choice probabilities and dynamics.
2. We establish pathwise differentiability for these functionals and derive their efficient influence
functions, yielding the corresponding semiparametric efficiency bounds.
3. We construct automatic debiased machine learning estimators (Chernozhukov et al., 2022;
van der Laan et al., 2025a) that accommodate flexible nonparametric nuisance estimation
and achieve √n-consistency, asymptotic normality, and semiparametric efficiency.
4. We apply our framework to obtain efficiency bounds and efficient estimators for several key
IRL estimands, including the value of a fixed policy, the value of a softmax policy, and the
value of a policy under a normalized reward.
Our work extends inference methods for parametric dynamic discrete choice (DDC) models in
economics (Rust, 1987; Hotz and Miller, 1993; Aguirregabiria and Mira, 2010) to settings with
nonparametric reward representations and modern, machine-learning-based nuisance estimation.
Unlike classical estimators—such as the nested fixed-point method of Rust (1987), the two-step
conditional choice probability approach of Hotz and Miller (1993), and the simulation-based es-
timators of Hotz et al. (1994)—our framework avoids repeated dynamic programming or Monte
Carlo simulation and the delicate numerical optimization these procedures typically require. In
contrast, we show that relevant reward-dependent estimands in DDC can be naturally expressed
as smooth functionals of the log behaviour policy and the transition kernel. For instance, we show
that normalized reward functions, such as the outside-option normalization of Rust (1987), can be
recovered using fitted Q-iteration applied to the log behaviour policy. Similarly, for policy value
functionals under a normalized reward, our efficient estimator relies only on learning the behaviour
policy via probabilistic classification, using fitted Q-iteration to recover the normalized reward and
value function, and estimating the discounted occupancy ratio nuisances, all of which are standard
nuisance components in reinforcement learning. Consequently, our framework provides new tools
for inference that complement and extend existing methods in both the IRL and DDC literatures.
3

1.2
Related work
Dynamic discrete choice models. Existing econometric work on DDC and dynamic games is
predominantly parametric, focusing on identification and efficient estimation of finite-dimensional
structural reward (utility) parameters.
These approaches do not allow for fully nonparametric
reward models or target the reward-dependent value functionals—such as policy values and value
differences—central to IRL. To our knowledge, there is no existing derivation of efficient influence
functions for such functionals in softmax IRL or in DDC models with nonparametric rewards, nor
any debiased, machine-learning-based inference framework for this setting.
While identification and semiparametric efficiency have been studied extensively in DDC mod-
els, this work concerns largely parametric utility specifications. For dynamic discrete games, Bajari
et al. (2015) derive identification and efficiency results when per-period rewards are parametrized by
a finite-dimensional vector, treating equilibrium objects and transition probabilities nonparamet-
rically. Foundational identification results include Hotz and Miller (1993), Hotz et al. (1994), and
Magnac and Thesmar (2002). Blundell and Horowitz (2007) develop nonparametric specification
tests for dynamic discrete choice models, and Blevins (2014) study nonparametric identification
of dynamic decision processes with discrete and continuous choices. Other work addresses latent
heterogeneity or shock distributions: Kasahara and Shimotsu (2009) analyze identification with
unobserved heterogeneity, Buchinsky et al. (2010) propose a diagnostic for zero semiparametric in-
formation in fixed-effects DDC models, and Buchholz et al. (2021) consider models with unspecified
shock distributions. Additional semiparametric results include Arcidiacono and Miller (2011) and
Norets and Tang (2014). A complementary line of research studies identification of counterfactu-
als under minimal structure. Kalouptsidi et al. (2021) show that certain counterfactuals may be
point- or set-identified even when utilities are not. Other identification results include Abbring and
Daljord (2020) on discount-factor identification and Kalouptsidi et al. (2017) on nonidentification
of counterfactuals in dynamic games. These papers consider broader DDC settings but do not yield
the exact identification of value differences or normalized rewards that arises under the softmax
(Gumbel-shock) framework we impose. Following Rust (1987), we adopt the widely used Gumbel
specification; extending our analysis to more general shock distributions is an interesting direction
for future work.
Inverse reinforcement learning. Classical IRL methods recover parametric or linear rewards
under hard optimality assumptions (e.g. Ng and Russell, 2000; Abbeel and Ng, 2004), while more
recent approaches use maximum-entropy or adversarial formulations with flexible function approx-
imators (e.g. Ziebart et al., 2008; Levine et al., 2011; Ho and Ermon, 2016; Fu et al., 2018; Geng
et al., 2020). Within this line of work, Deep PQR (Geng et al., 2020) shows that, in softmax IRL
and DDC models, an anchor-action constraint allows recovery of a particular normalized reward
from the behavior policy. Our companion paper van der Laan et al. (2025c) further develops this
perspective by characterizing the solution and identification structure, providing new fixed-point
characterizations of normalized rewards, and establishing point identification of policy value differ-
ences. Related work on identifiability in MaxEnt IRL includes Cao et al. (2021), who characterize
the full equivalence class of rewards consistent with a given softmax policy. These methods, however,
4

focus on reward estimation and do not provide formal statistical inference for reward-dependent
functionals.
Off-policy evaluation and double reinforcement learning.
Our approach is also related
to debiased and doubly robust methods for off-policy evaluation, which use DML techniques to
estimate policy values when the reward function is known (e.g. Jiang and Li, 2016; Thomas and
Brunskill, 2016; Tang et al., 2019; Liu et al., 2018; van Der Laan et al., 2018; Kallus and Uehara,
2020; Uehara et al., 2020; Shi et al., 2021; Kallus and Uehara, 2022; Mehrabi and Wager, 2024).
A closely related development is Double Reinforcement Learning (DRL), which studies debiased
and statistically efficient estimation of policy values and other functionals of the Q-function under
a known reward (Kallus and Uehara, 2020, 2022; van der Laan et al., 2025b). Debiased IRL is
complementary to these methods: off-policy evaluation and DRL take the reward as known and
seek inference for policy values, whereas we treat the reward as unknown and impose a softmax
behavioural model to recover it.
2
Preliminaries
2.1
Problem setup
We consider an agent making decisions in an infinite-horizon, time-homogeneous Markov decision
process (MDP). At time 0, the agent starts in an initial state S0 ∈S drawn from a density ρ0 with
respect to a measure µ on the state space S. At each time t ∈N ∪{0}, the agent occupies a state
St ∈S, selects an action At ∈A according to its policy π0(· | St), and receives an immediate reward
r†
0(At, St), where r†
0 : A × S →R is an unknown, time-invariant reward function. The next state
St+1 is drawn from a time-invariant distribution k0(s′ | At, St) µ(ds′), where the transition kernel
k0(s′ | a, s) specifies the conditional density of St+1 = s′ given (At, St) = (a, s) with respect to µ. In
the IRL setting, the rewards {r†
0(At, St)}∞
t=0 are observed by the agent but hidden from the analyst;
only the states and actions are available. We let P0 denote the joint distribution of the initial
transition (S0, A0, S1) and P0 the induced distribution of the full trajectory (S0, A0, S1, A1, . . .).
For convenience, we write f0 for any summary fP0 depending on P0, such as E0 for expectations
under P0.
The inferential task in IRL is to learn features of the unknown reward function r†
0 from observed
state–action transitions, such as policy values. We observe Dn := {(Si, Ai, S′
i)}n
i=1, a collection of
n i.i.d. samples from P0. Although only one transition is observed per individual, datasets with
multiple transitions can be decomposed into such units, and temporal dependence can be handled
using Markov chain limit theory (Bibaut et al., 2021; Mehrabi and Wager, 2024). A fundamental
challenge is that r†
0 is not point identified from state–action data alone: many distinct rewards
induce the same observable behaviour (Ng et al., 1999; Ng and Russell, 2000).
IRL methods
address this nonidentification by imposing an optimality model that links the behaviour policy to
the reward. In the next subsection, we introduce the soft optimality framework that underpins our
analysis.
5

2.2
Soft optimality and the soft Bellman equation
We adopt the notion of soft optimality, which arises in Gumbel–shock-to-reward formulations
(Rust, 1987; Hotz and Miller, 1993; Aguirregabiria and Mira, 2010) and in maximum-entropy IRL
(Ziebart et al., 2008, 2010; Haarnoja et al., 2017). Soft optimality relaxes deterministic, reward-
maximizing behavior by introducing entropy regularization into the control objective, yielding
stochastic softmax decision rules that balance reward and exploration. The agent maximizes an
entropy-regularized expected return, and the resulting optimal value function is characterized by
the soft Bellman equation.
To introduce soft optimality formally, we first review the classical (hard) value and Q-functions.
For any policy π : A×S →[0, 1] and transition kernel k : S×A×S →R, let Ek,π denote expectation
with respect to trajectories generated by the MDP with transition kernel k, policy π, and initial
state density ρ0. For any function f : A × S →R, define the forward operators

Entropy regularization augments the reward r0(At, St) with an entropy bonus τ H(π(· | St)),

referred to as the soft value and Q-functions (Haarnoja et al., 2017). Since rewards are identified
only up to scale, we set τ = 1 without loss of generality, interpreting rewards on a relative scale.

Soft optimality also arises naturally in structural models of agent behavior based on Gumbel-
shock specifications. In dynamic discrete-choice (DDC) models (Rust, 1987; Hotz and Miller, 1993;

or may appear stochastic to the analyst while being fully optimal with respect to an augmented
state that includes these shocks (Rust, 1987; Pitombeira-Neto et al., 2024).

3
Identification
3.1
Partial identification of the reward
The reward function r†
0 is only partially identified without additional constraints: it is determined
only up to the class of reward functions that induce the same softmax policy for a soft-optimal
agent (Cao et al., 2021). Its scale is also unidentified; by convention, this is fixed by normalizing the
entropy-regularization parameter to τ = 1, which is equivalent to fixing the softmax temperature
(Haarnoja et al., 2017). We first formalize this partial identification. In the next subsections, we
show that although the reward is only partially identified, policy value differences remain point
identified, and that the reward becomes point identified under an additional normalization con-
straint. These results build on identification arguments in our companion paper van der Laan et al.
(2025c) and related work (Fu et al., 2018; Geng et al., 2020; Cao et al., 2021), and they form the
basis for the inferential theory developed below.
Formally, the partial identification of r†
0 can be characterized through the softmax likelihood
(Rust, 1987; Ziebart et al., 2008). The observed policy π0 maximizes this soft Bellman–constrained
likelihood, so any reward r that induces the same softmax policy is also a solution of the population
M-estimation problem

3.2
Identification of policy values
Although the reward is only partially identified, many quantities of practical interest remain point
identified. Lemma 1 shows that behavior determines r†
0 only up to potential-based shaping. Such
shifts change all policy values by the same constant and thus do not affect policy comparisons.
Consequently, policy value differences remain identified, and the log–behavior-policy reward r0
suffices for policy evaluation. The next result formalizes this fact and restates Theorem 1 of van der
Laan et al. (2025c).
Theorem 1 (Behavior policy identifies value differences). Suppose that r†
0 solves (4) for a discount
factor γ ∈[0, 1). Then, for any policy π,

A subtle but important point is that the identification of policy value differences holds only for
the discount factor γ that appears in the soft Bellman equation governing the agent’s behavior. To
evaluate policies under a different discount factor γ′, the reward must typically be point identified.
Section 3.3 shows how this can be achieved via a normalization constraint. Before turning to that
result, we illustrate Theorem 1 in a counterfactual softmax setting.

3.3
Exact reward identification under normalization
The next theorem shows that, once a normalization constraint is imposed, the true reward r†
0 can
be recovered exactly from the log–behavior policy reward r0. To achieve point identification, we
fix a reference policy ν and impose the normalization
νr†
0(s) :=
X
˜a∈A
ν(˜a | s) r†
0(˜a, s) = 0,
P0-a.e. s.
(9)
Intuitively, this condition anchors the otherwise free state potential in (6).
In discrete–choice
applications, a common convention is to fix the reward of a designated “outside option” to zero
or, alternatively, to impose that the rewards across actions sum to zero. The former corresponds
to choosing ν(a | s) = 1{a = a⋆}, a point mass on a reference action a⋆, while the latter arises by
taking ν(a | s) = 1/|A|, the uniform distribution over A. For example, in the engine–replacement
model of Rust (1987), not replacing the engine yields zero payoff, and in healthcare, continuing the
current treatment may likewise be normalized to yield zero payoff, whereas switching treatments
may incur an immediate cost.
The following result provides a streamlined formulation of van der Laan et al. (2025c, Theo-
rem 2), showing that identification of the normalized reward can be expressed directly through the
Q-function qν,γ

soft optimality equation (4) reduces to estimating the behaviour policy via probabilistic classifica-
tion and solving a single linear fixed-point equation for the corresponding Q-function, in contrast
to earlier IRL and DDC approaches that relied on nested fixed-point iterations or dynamic pro-
gramming (Rust, 1987; Ziebart et al., 2008).
While we focus on linear normalizations determined by ν, other choices are possible. One may

instance, the pseudo-reward r0 corresponds to imposing log P

4
Debiased Inverse Reinforcement Learning
4.1
Statistical objective
The identification results in Section 3 imply that the log–behavior policy r0 = log π0 can be used
as a pseudo-reward for many IRL targets. For instance, policy value differences—central to policy
evaluation—are identified directly from r0 by Theorem 1. Moreover, under a normalization con-
straint, Theorem 2 shows that the true reward r†
0 and any of its functionals can also be recovered
from r0.
Building on this perspective, our goal is to infer a real-valued summary ψ0 ∈R of the pseudo-
reward r0 and transition kernel k0, of the form
ψ0 := E0[m(A, S, r0, k0)],
where (r, k) 7→m(·, r, k) is a smooth map from H × K to L2(λ). Formally, ψ0 = Ψ(P0) corresponds

In the remainder of this work, we develop semiparametric efficiency theory for Ψ, derive its
efficient influence function, and use this theory to construct automatic debiased machine learning
(autoDML) estimators for ψ0. Before presenting the general framework, we introduce motivating
examples from policy evaluation in IRL, which will serve as running illustrations throughout.
4.2
Examples for policy evaluation
Our framework accommodates a wide range of counterfactual targets in IRL. We present high-level
examples here to illustrate the structure of the target functionals; these examples are revisited in
full detail in Section 6.
As a first example, consider the value of a given policy π under the pseudo-reward r0. By
Theorem 1, value differences under r0 coincide with those under the true reward r†
0, so r0 suffices
for policy evaluation. In many applications, the value under r0 is itself the parameter of inter-
est, because r0 is typically the reward-like quantity learned from data and subsequently used for
downstream planning or control. For instance, in robotics a pseudo-reward may be learned from
human demonstrations and then used to evaluate or optimize policies in simulated environments
(e.g. Abbeel and Ng, 2004; Ziebart et al., 2008; Fu et al., 2018).
Example 1a (Policy value). The policy value ψ0 := E0[V π,γ

4.3
Statistical efficiency and functional bias expansion
A key step in our methodology is to establish the pathwise differentiability of Ψ and derive its
efficient influence function (EIF). In semiparametric statistics, the EIF determines the generalized
Cram´er–Rao lower bound for Ψ and forms the basis for constructing debiased, efficient estimators
(Bickel et al., 1993; Laan and Robins, 2003; Van der Laan et al., 2011; Chernozhukov et al., 2018).
The following condition ensures smoothness of the map (r, k) 7→m(·, ·, r, k). The reward tangent
space TH is defined as the completion of H under the inner product

for additive reward noise, the EIF for continuous linear functionals of the Q-function is unchanged,
since the noise component contributes only an orthogonal nuisance score (Bickel et al., 1993; Laan
and Robins, 2003).

5
Automatic debiased estimation and inference
5.1
General form of the estimator
Let rn and kn denote estimators of the pseudo-reward r0 (the log behavior policy) and the transition
kernel k0, respectively. The pseudo-reward r0 can be learned from state–action data {(Si, Ai)}n
i=1
via probabilistic classification, while k0 may be estimated using conditional density methods. A
natural estimator of ψ0 is the plug-in estimator n−1 Pn
i=1 m(Ai, Si, rn, kn).
However, when rn
and kn are obtained through flexible, nonparametric methods, this plug-in approach can exhibit
substantial bias and slow convergence. To address this, we propose automatic debiased machine
learning (autoDML) estimators that incorporate an influence-function correction.
Let αn ∈H and βn ∈L2(P0) denote estimators of α0 and β0 from Section 4.3. We assume that
βn is centered with respect to µ, so that
R
βn(s′, a, s) µ(ds′) = 0, which can always be enforced post
hoc. Our autoDML estimator is the one-step estimator

which can be written explicitly as

estimator (Bickel et al., 1993). The representers α0 and β0 can be estimated automatically via
the minimum-loss representations in (13) and (14), which require only the partial derivatives of
(r, k) 7→E0[m(A, S, r, k)]. Because the structure of (15) is agnostic to the specific choice of m, we
refer to this estimator as automatic (Chernozhukov et al., 2022; van der Laan et al., 2025a).
When ψ0 and the EIF component β0/k0 depend on quantities derived from k0 (e.g., value or
Q-functions), it is often unnecessary to estimate k0 directly. Instead, one can estimate the relevant
17

nuisances—such as value and Q-functions—via iterative regression methods like fitted Q-iteration.
For the EIF correction to remain valid, however, the von Mises expansion of Theorem 4 must be
re-expressed in terms of these nuisances, with the remainder shown to be second order in their
estimation errors. This holds for the policy-value parameters in our examples, as shown in the next
section. Before doing so, we establish the asymptotic theory for ψn.
5.2
Asymptotic theory
The next theorem establishes that the proposed estimator ψn is asymptotically linear with influence
function χ0 and is therefore efficient for ψ0. We impose the following conditions.
(C4) (Sample splitting) The estimators rn, kn, αn, and βn are obtained from data independent of
Dn.
(C5) (Boundedness) ∥φrn,kn(· ; αn, βn)∥L2(P0) = OP (1) and ∥φr0,k0(· ; α0, β0)∥L2(P0) = O(1).
(C6) (Consistency) ∥φrn,kn(· ; αn, βn) −φr0,k0(· ; α0, β0)∥L2(P0) = oP (1).
(C7) (Nuisance estimation rates)
(a) ∥rn −r0∥H = oP (n−1/4).
(b)

The conditions above are standard in debiased machine learning. Condition C4 enforces inde-
pendence between nuisance estimation and evaluation; this can be relaxed to cross-fitting, where
the sample is partitioned and nuisance functions are estimated on held-out folds (Van der Laan
et al., 2011; Chernozhukov et al., 2018).
Condition C5 guarantees uniform L2 boundedness of
the true and estimated influence functions. Condition C6 requires consistency of the estimated
influence function, typically ensured when the nuisance estimators are consistent. Condition C7
specifies the convergence rates needed for the von Mises remainder to be second order. In par-
ticular, part (c) yields a form of partial double robustness: slower convergence of αn can be
offset by faster convergence of rn.

Applications to policy evaluation
6.1
Parameters for policy evaluation
In this section, we apply our framework to the three policy–evaluation targets introduced in Sec-
tion 4.2: values of a fixed policy, values of a softmax policy, and values under reward normalization.
Although Theorem 3 shows that the EIF is obtained once the Riesz representers α0 and β0 of the
partial derivatives of (r, k) 7→E0[m(A, S, r, k)] are identified, and Theorem 4 reduces the von Mises
analysis to controlling the linearization remainder Rem0(r0, k0; r, k), carrying out these steps in
concrete cases requires nontrivial analysis. For example, Example 1b involves differentiating the
solution of a soft Bellman equation—a nonlinear inverse problem—via implicit functional differenti-
ation (see Appendix F.2). For each parameter class, we derive the appropriate von Mises expansion,
establish its doubly robust structure, and construct specialized DML estimators that avoid direct
estimation of k0 by instead targeting higher-level nuisance components such as Q-functions.
We begin by reviewing and providing notation for the policy evaluation parameters introduced
in Section 4.2.
Example 1a (Policy value). The policy value ψ0 := E0[V π,γ

6.2
Efficient influence functions for policy value parameters
We now turn to establishing pathwise differentiability and deriving efficient influence functions for
the policy value parameters introduced above. The variance of each EIF yields the semiparametric
efficiency bound for the corresponding parameter, and these EIFs form the basis for constructing
efficient estimators in the next section. For presentation, we state only the EIFs in the theorem
statements; the proofs in Appendix E.2 provide the precise forms of the Riesz representers α0 and
β0.

6.3
Estimators and asymptotic theory
We now construct example-specific DML estimators that avoid direct estimation of the transition
kernel k0 by instead targeting the relevant higher-level nuisance functions. This reparameterization
reveals a doubly robust von Mises expansion and yields simplified rate conditions under which
Theorem 6 continues to apply.
Below we state only the required nuisance-rate conditions; the
remaining assumptions—analogous to C4–C6—are provided in Appendix A.2.

7
Conclusion
We developed a semiparametric framework for inference in softmax IRL and dynamic discrete choice
models with nonparametric rewards. By expressing a broad class of reward-dependent estimands as
smooth functionals of the behaviour policy and transition kernel, deriving their efficient influence
functions, and constructing automatic debiased ML estimators, the framework delivers valid and
computationally tractable inference without nested dynamic programming or parametric utility
restrictions. The resulting estimators rely only on standard supervised learning tools and fitted
Q-iteration, providing a simple alternative to classical dynamic programming and simulation-based
procedures. The framework also supplies general principles for deriving efficient influence functions
and constructing semiparametrically efficient estimators for new targets. In this way, it unifies and
extends classical inference for Gumbel-shock DDC models and modern machine-learning-based IRL
methods, offering a reusable template for principled statistical inference under the shared softmax
structure.
Several directions remain open.
First, our analysis adopts the Gumbel-shock structure un-
derlying the softmax policy.
Extending the framework to generalized Gumbel families or fully
nonparametric shock distributions would permit inference under weaker behavioural assumptions.
Second, while we focus on linear normalizations indexed by a reference policy ν, the same machinery
should extend to affine or nonlinear normalizations. Finally, extending the analysis to nonhomoge-
neous MDPs, finite-horizon settings, or dependence across transitions presents another promising
direction.
References
Pieter Abbeel and Andrew Y. Ng. Apprenticeship learning via inverse reinforcement learning. In
ICML, 2004.
Jaap H. Abbring and Øystein Daljord. Identifying the discount factor in dynamic discrete choice
models. Quantitative Economics, 11(2):471–501, 2020.
Victor Aguirregabiria and Pedro Mira.
Dynamic discrete choice structural models: A survey.
Journal of Econometrics, 156(1):38–67, 2010.
Peter Arcidiacono and Robert A. Miller. Conditional choice probability estimation of dynamic
discrete choice models with unobserved heterogeneity. Econometrica, 79(6):1823–1867, 2011.

Proofs for Section 4.3
The following lemma shows that rP is a solution to a moment-matching problem. In the main text,
we set H := L∞(λ). For generality, the lemma below allows H ⊆L∞(λ) to be a linear subspace
34

that includes the state-only functions, i.e., L∞(µ). For example, H may correspond to an additive
or partially linear model.

contains all state-only functions, i.e., L∞(µ). Let rP denote the solution to (18) for this choice of

Lemma 5. Assume (r, k) 7→E0[m(A, S, r, k)] is Fr´echet differentiable at (r0, k0); that is,
E0[m(A, S, r0 + h, k0 + g) −m(A, S, r0, k0) −∂rm(A, S, r0, k0)(h) −∂km(A, S, r0, k0)(g)] = o(∥h∥H,0 + ∥g∥K,0)
as (h, g) →(0, 0) in the product norm ∥h∥H,0 + ∥g∥K,0. Then Conditions C1-C3 hold.
proof of Lemma 5. Gˆateaux differentiability in Condition C1 follows immediately from Fr´echet dif-
ferentiability, and the boundedness of the derivative functionals in Condition C2 also follows from
Fr´echet differentiability. Let F(r, k) := E0[m(A, S, r, k)]. By the triangle inequality and bounded-
ness of the derivatives,
F(r0 + h, k0 + g) −F(r0, k0)
 = |E0[∂rm(A, S, r0, k0)(h) + ∂km(A, S, r0, k0)(g)]| + o(∥h∥H,r0 + ∥g∥K,0)
= O(∥h∥H,r0 + ∥g∥K,0) + o(∥h∥H,r0 + ∥g∥K,0) .
Thus, by the definition of the o(·) term, for any ε > 0 there exists δ > 0 such that whenever
∥h∥H,r0 + ∥g∥K,0 < δ,
|F(r0 + h, k0 + g) −F(r0, k0)| ≤(Cr + Ck + ε) (∥h∥H,r0 + ∥g∥K,0),
which establishes local Lipschitz continuity at (r0, k0).

Proof of Theorem 6. Write ψn = Pnφn, where we define the estimated uncentered influence func-

where χ0 is the EIF in Theorem 3. Then

= Pn(φ0 −P0φ0) + Pn(φn −φ0)
= Pnχ0 + (Pn −P0)(φn −φ0) + {P0φn −ψ0}.

Lemma 7 (Invertibility of the Bellman operator under stationarity overlap). Assume γ < 1 and
D1. Then Tk0,π : L2(λ) →L2(λ) admits a bounded inverse, both as an operator on L2(λ) and on
L2(π0 ⊗ρ0).
Proof. Recall that pπ,γ
k0 is the stationary state µ-density for the counterfactual MDP with policy π
and kernel k0. We claim that Tk0,π is a γ-contraction in L2(π ⊗pπ,γ
k0 ). Let Pk0,π denote the Markov
(transition) operator under k0 and π, acting on state-only functions by
Pk0,πf(s) :=
Z
f(s′) k0(ds′ | s, a) π(da | s),
so that Tk0,π := I −γ Pk0,π.
For any f ∈L2(pstat
k0,π), Jensen’s inequality gives
∥Pk0,πf∥2
L2(pstat
k0,π) = ES∼pstat
k0,π
h E[f(S′) | S]
2i
≤ES∼pstat
k0,π

E
 f(S′)2 | S

= ES′∼pstat
k0,π

f(S′)2
= ∥f∥2
L2(pstat
k0,π),
where all expectations are taken under the stationary distribution pstat
k0,π, and the third equality
follows from stationarity of the chain. Hence ∥Pk0,π∥L2(pstat
k0,π)→L2(pstat
k0,π) ≤1, and therefore
∥γ Pk0,π∥L2(pstat
k0,π)→L2(pstat
k0,π) ≤γ < 1.
Thus Tk0,π is a perturbation of the identity by a γ-contraction with γ < 1. By Banach’s inversion
theorem, Tk0,π is therefore invertible on L2(π ⊗pπ,γ
k0 ).
By D1, the spaces L2(π ⊗pπ,γ
k0 ) and L2(π0 ⊗ρ0) are equivalent, with norms that are uniformly
comparable. Hence Tk0,π is also invertible on L2(π0 ⊗ρ0). Similarly, by the assumptions in Section
2.3, both π0 and ρ0 are uniformly bounded above and below. It follows that L2(π0 ⊗ρ0) and L2(λ)
are equivalent in norm and as spaces. Therefore Tk0,π is also invertible on L2(λ).
□

Proof of Theorem 8. By Lemma 11, the map (r, k) 7→E0[m(A, S, r, k)] is Fr´echet differentiable in
the supremum norm and, hence, Gˆateaux differentiable over r ∈H and k ∈K. The derivatives
are provided in Lemma 11 and are stated later in this proof. By D2 and Lemma 7, applied with
π = π⋆
r0,k0, the operator Tr0,k0 : L2(λ) →L2(λ) admits a bounded inverse, both on L2(λ) and on
L2(π0 ⊗ρ0). As a consequence, the partial derivatives in the sup-norm space,

and

r,k exists, and the linearization

F
Smoothness of the Soft Bellman Equation and Its Solutions
In this section, we establish several technical lemmas on the smoothness of the soft Bellman equation
and its solutions. Some of these results are used in deriving the EIF and the von Mises expansion
for Example 1b. Other results are included for completeness.
F.1

We study the functional smoothness of the soft Bellman equation and its fixed-point solution,
taking the softmax entropy parameter to be 1. Let λ := # ⊗µ denote the product measure on

on S. For any measurable B ⊆A × S,

F.2
Technical lemmas
We begin with the following lemma, which ensures that the map (r, k) 7→vr,k is well defined.
Lemma 16 (Existence and uniqueness of solution to soft Bellman equation). For each r ∈L∞(λ)
and k ∈K, there exists a unique solution vr,k ∈L∞(λ) to the soft Bellman equation Fk(r, v) = 0
over v ∈L∞(λ).
Proof. For every r ∈L∞(λ), the map v 7→v −Fk(r, v) is a contraction on L∞(λ). By Banach’s
87

fixed-point theorem, there exists a unique vr,k ∈L∞(λ) such that vr,k = vr,k −Fk(r, vr,k), and hence
Fk(r, vr,k) = 0.
□

Lemma 19 (Sup-norm Fr´echet differentiability of the soft value function). Let (r, k) ∈H × K.

since γ < 1, the map F is Fr´echet differentiable as a map L∞(λ) × L∞(λ) × N →L∞(λ), and
its derivative depends jointly and continuously on (r, v, k). Moreover, by Condition ??, the partial
derivative
∂vFk(r, vr,k) : L∞(λ) →L∞(λ)
is a bounded linear isomorphism. The implicit function theorem for Banach spaces therefore implies
